You are the argument-development stage in an auditable publication pipeline.

PROJECT
{
  "publication_name": "Agent Signal",
  "topic": "Evidence-based adoption of AI coding agents by small software teams",
  "audience": "Technical leads and hands-on engineering managers at software teams of roughly 2–20 people who understand pull requests, CI, testing, and code review",
  "central_question": "Which new coding-agent capabilities are genuinely production-ready, what evidence supports the claims, and what should a small software team do next?"
}

EDITORIAL POLICY
# Agent Signal Editorial Policy — Version 1

## Purpose and scope

Agent Signal helps technical leads at small software teams decide whether and how to adopt AI coding agents. We cover changes that affect production engineering decisions: delegation, context and harness design, verification, security, cost, team workflow, and maintenance. We exclude generic AI news, model-release scorekeeping without workflow implications, beginner prompting tutorials, investment speculation, and claims whose publication date or supporting passage cannot be verified.

## Selection criteria

Assess every eligible item at the level of the claims we may use, not by publisher prestige or model confidence.

1. **Audience relevance:** Prefer evidence that can change a small team's adoption, workflow, controls, or near-term watch list.
2. **Freshness:** Supporting material must fall within the mechanical 30-date window. A refreshed page or re-upload does not make an old event new. State what is newly reported when recent material discusses older events.
3. **Claim-level credibility:** Look for an inspectable method, direct observation, reproducible artifact, named example, or precise limitation. Institutional reputation and popularity are context, not proof.
4. **Novelty:** Prefer genuinely new capabilities, measurements, failure modes, or practitioner lessons. Repeated coverage of one announcement is one underlying claim.
5. **Perspective diversity:** Build a mix of vendor capability claims, independent research, and practitioner experience. Do not let one company or distribution channel define the story.

## Evidence and incentives

- Treat product announcements and vendor benchmarks as evidence of what a vendor says or ships, not independent proof of effectiveness.
- Record relevant incentives: a vendor sells the featured tool; an investor promotes a portfolio thesis; a creator benefits from attention; a researcher may be working with a bounded sample or benchmark.
- Corroboration requires evidence that is independent in origin. Syndication, reposts, reactions to the same press release, and a podcast's YouTube copy do not count separately.
- Prefer exact passages and timestamped transcript segments. Reject unsupported numerical claims and do not infer causation from usage or activity metrics.

## Uncertainty and disagreement

- Distinguish reported facts, attributed source claims, and our interpretation.
- When credible sources conflict, explain differences in task, sample, measurement, incentives, or operating conditions before deciding whether there is a real contradiction.
- Use calibrated language. Say “suggests,” “in this sample,” or “the vendor reports” when the evidence does not justify a general conclusion.
- Do not manufacture balance. A limitation or counterargument must be supported by evidence or by a clearly stated boundary of the available evidence.
- Defer an item when it may become useful but lacks a transcript, date, inspectable support, or enough context. Reject it when it is irrelevant, duplicative, promotional without usable evidence, or substantively unreliable.

## Series roles

- **Piece 1 — What changed:** Select consequential developments and explain why they matter now.
- **Piece 2 — What survives scrutiny:** Compare capability claims with research, verification needs, and practitioner experience.
- **Piece 3 — What to do next:** Derive bounded actions and watch signals for small teams without presenting speculation as fact.

Every accepted item must have a stated purpose in at least one piece. Each piece must use at least three independent publishers, contain a multi-source inference whose reasoning is explicit, and acknowledge a meaningful limitation.


STAGE INSTRUCTIONS
# Brief-development instructions

Create exactly three briefs with piece IDs `01`, `02`, and `03`. Use only accepted exact evidence versions assigned to the relevant piece.

Each brief must:

- answer its assigned editorial question with one contestable central argument;
- contain explicit, atomic claims typed as `reported_fact`, `source_claim`, or `inference`;
- attach every claim to an exact written quote or existing transcript segment;
- use at least three independent publishers;
- include at least one inference connecting evidence from multiple publishers and explain the reasoning in `synthesis`;
- explain commercial or methodological incentives when relevant;
- state a meaningful limitation or counterargument; and
- avoid turning missing evidence into a confident conclusion.

The pieces must build on one another without repeating the same thesis: first establish what changed, then scrutinize claims and tensions, then derive bounded actions and watch signals for small teams.

What hasn't sped up is everything after the PR goes up: making sure code is secure, watching the deploy, deciding whether a latency bump is real, figuring out which of eleven changes broke checkout.\n\nThat work is hard, repetitive, and needs a lot of context. Sounds like the type of work that bots should be doing!\n\nWe've said we're building toward self-driving codebases. When it finds a regression, it tells you which change it suspects and what it plans to do.     "title": "Improved token efficiency for longer agent runs",
    "url": "https://cursor.com/blog/improved-token-efficiency",
    "published_at": "2026-09-23T00:00:00Z",
    "text": "As agents have matured and learned to tackle more ambitious tasks, token spend has shifted. Agents now work for longer and carry more context from one step to the next, making the way we assemble and manage that context increasingly important.\n\nWhere agent inference spend goes\n\nProduction traffic · width = share of total spend · shade = billing type\n\nWhere agent inference spend goes\n\nProduction traffic · width = share of total spend · shade = billing type\n\nWhere agent inference spend goes\n\nProduction traffic · width = share of total spend · shade = billing type\n\nNotes: System & tool defs includes compaction summaries. User text includes manually attached skills. Skills & plugins includes skill descriptions, MCP tool descriptions, and rules that go in static context.\n\nOver the past few months we've responded to this shift by improving the efficiency of Cursor's agent harness. The harness gives us direct control over how each request is assembled, how context is reused, and when work is divided across agents. Changes across each of these layers reduced token costs for users by 7% without reducing agent quality.\n\n# Trimming the system prompt\n\nEvery agent turn includes context supplied by Cursor before the model begins working. This includes the system prompt and definitions for the tools the agent can use. Because this context is included throughout a conversation, it had become one of the largest sources of spend that we fully control.\n\nWhen models were less capable, we had to spell out instructions for tool usage, task management, and code-change workflows. We also had to guard against strange behaviors like extremely long hash dumps, binary output, and emojis.\n\nAs models improved, much of that direction became unnecessary. Instead of long lists of \"DO NOT do this,\" \"You must,\" or \"Important\" instructions, we could simply define how a tool behaves and models would generally comply. This was true across model families, allowing us to trim roughly 66% of our system prompt.\n\nOver time, we continue to add and remove instructions as new models require new guidance, which then flows into the training of future models. Leveraging A/B tests on a large user base is crucial to effectively optimizing the harness for real traffic. While evals can be a fast and useful proxy, they often represent \"hard\" problems and don't properly reflect the true distribution of user requests.\n\n# Loading tools only when needed\n\nThe system prompt is only one part of the context Cursor supplies on every turn. Another is tool definitions, which had grown dramatically over the course of the year as we added more powerful capabilities to the Cursor agent, including background shell monitoring, cloud subagents, and more reliable access to web content. Most of these tools are important, but each is needed in fewer than 20% of conversations.\n\nThat created an opportunity to improve efficiency by keeping tools available without including their full definitions in every request. We'd solved a similar problem earlier this year when we moved MCP tools into dynamic context, loading them only when needed. This reduced total tokens by 46.9% across sessions that called an MCP tool.\n\nWe have now applied the same technique to our own built-in tools.\n\nTo decide which tools to keep in static context, we A/B tested several configurations based on how often each tool was used and whether models needed to see it from the start. We tracked token usage, cost, latency, tool-call errors, and overall agent usage to make sure the savings did not degrade quality.\n\nMost commonly invoked tools\n\nShare of agent conversations invoking each tool at least once\n\nMost commonly invoked tools\n\nShare of agent conversations invoking each tool at least once\n\nMost commonly invoked tools\n\nShare of agent conversations invoking each tool at least once\n\nUltimately, we kept the high-frequency tools for reading, searching, editing, and using the shell in static context. We also retained ask_question , which some models tended to hallucinate calls for, and tools that are crucial to specific product flows, such as create_plan in Plan Mode. The remaining tools now load when the agent needs them.\n\nOffloading built-in tools cut static-context description tokens by 60%\n\nKept in static context\n\nOffloaded to dynamic context\n\nOffloading built-in tools cut static-context description tokens by 60%\n\nKept in static context\n\nOffloaded to dynamic context\n\nOffloading built-in tools cut static-context description tokens by 60%\n\nKept in static context\n\nOffloaded to dynamic context\n\n# Improving cache reuse\n\nAfter reducing the amount of static context in each request, we improved how effectively repeated context could be cached across turns.\n\nEvery agent turn resends a long request containing tools, system instructions, setup, and the conversation so far. Much of the beginning stays the same from one turn to the next, while the conversation at the end continues to grow.\n\nPrompt caching allows the model provider to reuse that unchanged prefix. However, caching configurability can vary by provider. Before GPT-5.6, the cache boundary was determined automatically based on the latest request. Even though tools and system instructions rarely changed, they were not cleanly marked as reusable on their own.\n\nSince GPT-5.6, the OpenAI API allows clients to mark explicit cache breakpoints alongside its default implicit caching. We now place breakpoints after stable layers of the request and before the growing conversation, allowing later turns to reuse more of the unchanged prefix.\n\nBreakpoints only help if the prefix itself stays stable, so we also tightened what sits at the front of each request. We did this by reserving tools and system instructions for content that rarely changes, and by moving more variable setup past the cache boundaries into our \"phantom user message.\" This holds user- and request-specific context like skills, subagents, and environment info.\n\nThese changes reduced the rate of cold cache misses by 20%.\n\n# Compressing file reads\n\nAnother large source of token spend is the context an agent adds as it works, much of which comes from reading files.\n\nCursor's agent reads files through a Read tool, which traditionally numbered every line because models are not good at counting lines on their own and need to cite specific sections for the user.\n\nA single line number uses only around three to five tokens, but when an agent reads tens of thousands of lines during a session, numbering every one adds a meaningful amount of context.\n\nWe reduced that overhead by including line numbers only on every tenth line. This is still frequent enough for models to cite code properly, and the change reduced cache-read tokens by 1.6% with no reduction in quality.\n\n# Using subagents strategically\n\nLonger agent runs create more opportunities to delegate work to subagents. This can reduce token spend because each subagent typically starts with a fresh context window rather than carrying the parent agent's full conversation. Once it reports its results, the parent can continue without carrying the subagent's full working context.\n\nThis kind of context isolation between agents and subagents does carry a coordination tax, though, because agents that do not share context can duplicate work or pursue tasks that are no longer necessary.\n\nWe made two changes to capture the efficiency benefits without adding unnecessary coordination. First, we removed instructions that strongly encouraged agents to use subagents for codebase exploration. As subagents became more prevalent in training data and researchers incorporated them into post-training, models learned this pattern natively. Removing the extra prompting produced more balanced subagent usage.\n\nWe also tightened how subagents select models. Cursor can spawn subagents using any of our available models, which makes it possible to shore up blind spots across models or pair an expensive planning model with a cheaper one for implementation. We updated the tool arguments so agents choose a different model only when directed by the user or the harness.\n\n# Continuing to improve harness efficiency\n\nWe'll continue measuring how context accumulates across longer runs and testing where the harness can reduce repeated processing without affecting agent quality. Over time, we expect this will allow token use to grow far more slowly than the amount of work agents can complete. We've also taken these learnings to Grok Bot, where we're working to optimize its unique harness so that users can accomplish the most work at the lowest cost.\n\nHow we set up our cloud agent environment\n\nWhat we’ve learned building cloud agents\n\nIntroducing Grok 4.6\n\nAs agents have matured and learned to tackle more ambitious tasks, token spend has shifted. Agents now work for longer and carry more context from one step to the next, making the way we assemble and manage that context increasingly important.\n\nWhere agent inference spend goes\n\nProduction traffic · width = share of total spend · shade = billing type\n\nWhere agent inference spend goes\n\nProduction traffic · width = share of total spend · shade = billing type\n\nWhere agent inference spend goes\n\nProduction traffic · width = share of total spend · shade = billing type\n\nNotes: System & tool defs includes compaction summaries. User text includes manually attached skills. Skills & plugins includes skill descriptions, MCP tool descriptions, and rules that go in static context.\n\nOver the past few months we've responded to this shift by improving the efficiency of Cursor's agent harness. The harness gives us direct control over how each request is assembled, how context is reused, and when work is divided across agents. Changes across each of these layers reduced token costs for users by 7% without reducing agent quality.\n\n# Trimming the system prompt\n\nEvery agent turn includes context supplied by Cursor before the model begins working. This includes the system prompt and definitions for the tools the agent can use. Because this context is included throughout a conversation, it had become one of the largest sources of spend that we fully control.\n\nWhen models were less capable, we had to spell out instructions for tool usage, task management, and code-change workflows. We also had to guard against strange behaviors like extremely long hash dumps, binary output, and emojis.\n\nAs models improved, much of that direction became unnecessary. Instead of long lists of \"DO NOT do this,\" \"You must,\" or \"Important\" instructions, we could simply define how a tool behaves and models would generally comply. This was true across model families, allowing us to trim roughly 66% of our system prompt.\n\nOver time, we continue to add and remove instructions as new models require new guidance, which then flows into the training of future models. Leveraging A/B tests on a large user base is crucial to effectively optimizing the harness for real traffic. While evals can be a fast and useful proxy, they often represent \"hard\" problems and don't properly reflect the true distribution of user requests.\n\n# Loading tools only when needed\n\nThe system prompt is only one part of the context Cursor supplies on every turn. Another is tool definitions, which had grown dramatically over the course of the year as we added more powerful capabilities to the Cursor agent, including background shell monitoring, cloud subagents, and more reliable access to web content. Most of these tools are important, but each is needed in fewer than 20% of conversations.\n\nThat created an opportunity to improve efficiency by keeping tools available without including their full definitions in every request. We'd solved a similar problem earlier this year when we moved MCP tools into dynamic context, loading them only when needed. This reduced total tokens by 46.9% across sessions that called an MCP tool.\n\nWe have now applied the same technique to our own built-in tools.\n\nTo decide which tools to keep in static context, we A/B tested several configurations based on how often each tool was used and whether models needed to see it from the start. We tracked token usage, cost, latency, tool-call errors, and overall agent usage to make sure the savings did not degrade quality.\n\nMost commonly invoked tools\n\nShare of agent conversations invoking each tool at least once\n\nMost commonly invoked tools\n\nShare of agent conversations invoking each tool at least once\n\nMost commonly invoked tools\n\nShare of agent conversations invoking each tool at least once\n\nUltimately, we kept the high-frequency tools for reading, searching, editing, and using the shell in static context.     "item_id": "feed:independent-research-authors:258392c93310baab1471",
    "version_id": "v-b8c088105e97e9e6f3ce9ed9",

    "title": "AgentSWE: Can Coding Agents Build the Agent You Actually Want?",
    "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2610.13361",
    "published_at": "2026-10-09T10:35:27Z",
    "text": "AgentSWE: Can Coding Agents Build the Agent You Actually Want?\n\nAs the production of software passes into the hands of software itself, coding agents are increasingly commissioned to build agents fitted to their users’ own workflows. This delegation carries a quiet assumption, that the delivered agent will faithfully serve the requirement it was built for, rather than merely appear to. No existing benchmark tests that assumption. We introduce AgentSWE , a benchmark for agent software engineering spanning three stages of the agent development lifecycle: Creation of a complete agent from a natural-language requirement, Editing of production agent codebases, and Optimization toward a measurable target. Each of its 25 tasks is framed as a commission rather than an exam: the builder iterates against a handful of illustrative cases, while acceptance exercises the frozen submission on held-out cases designed to span the full requirement, grading behavior against requirement-derived rubrics under hard pass/fail gates or, for Optimization, against the source benchmark’s own objective. Evaluating a range of coding agents across models and harnesses, we find that none comes close to delivering what a requirement asks for: the gap between what a builder demonstrates and what it delivers is the norm, not the corner case. An accompanying empirical analysis of the failure trajectories identifies recurring failure modes, among them overfitting to the visible cases, hollow runtimes behind code that looks complete, and unsupported claims of completion, each a signal by which coding agents can learn to build the agents they are asked for.\n\nCoding agents such as Claude Code ( Anthropic, 2026 ) and Codex ( OpenAI, 2026a ) now carry substantial engineering workloads ( Chen et al., 2021 ; Yang et al., 2024b ; Wang et al., 2025b ) , and many of their users are not engineers: they describe what they need and delegate the rest ( Hitzig et al., 2026 ) . Increasingly, what they delegate is the building of an agent, a program that wraps a model, tools, and state around one recurring job so that the job runs on a fixed runtime, at a fixed cost, without a coding agent attending each run. Delegation of this kind rests on trust: that a coding agent, handed a requirement in natural language, will deliver the agent its user intends rather than one that merely appears to comply. Whether that trust is warranted is what this paper measures.\n\nAgent development spans three stages. It begins with creation , where a natural-language requirement must become a working system; continues through editing , where new capabilities join a living codebase without unsettling what already works; and matures into optimization , where the agent is pushed toward an explicit target, be it a public leaderboard or a production workload. Handing this lifecycle to coding agents invites failures subtler than broken code: an agent that passes visible examples yet misses the intent behind them ( Liu et al., 2023 ; Zhao et al., 2026a ; Lodkaew et al., 2026 ) ; a tool registered but never invoked ( Soni, 2026 ; Fan et al., 2026 ) ; a goal that drifts over a long horizon ( Arike et al., 2025 ; Menon et al., 2026 ) ; a report citing evidence no source contains ( Onweller et al., 2026 ; Rao et al., 2026 ) . Such failures are not hypothetical; they are the present condition of agent-built agents ( Raj et al., 2026 ; Naik et al., 2026 ) . The questions are how far coding agents compromise the requirements they are handed, on which capabilities, and for what reasons.\n\nThese questions outrun the instruments we have. SWE-bench and its descendants grade the repair of conventional software against a reference patch or test suite ( Jimenez et al., 2024 ; Chan et al., 2024 ) ; NL2Repo-Bench commissions whole repositories from requirement documents ( Ding et al., 2025 ) ; Paper2Code and PaperBench rebuild research implementations checked against the papers they reproduce ( Seo et al., 2025 ; Starace et al., 2025 ) . Each judges ordinary code against an answer known in advance. A commissioned agent wraps a stochastic policy around tools, answers to a requirement that has no reference solution, and keeps changing after delivery. Benchmarks that do evaluate agents, from AgentBench onward ( Liu et al., 2024 ) , examine a finished agent and ask how well it performs; none asks whether a coding agent could have built it. Work on agents that design or improve other agents ( Hu et al., 2025 ; Mishra et al., 2026 ) comes closest, but it measures how far a search procedure or a meta-agent can raise a benchmark score, not whether a general coding agent delivers the agent a requirement describes ( Section 2 ). Between judging built software and judging finished agents lies a step that current suites leave unmeasured: building the agent that a requirement asks for.\n\nWe introduce AgentSWE , a benchmark that turns faithful delegation from anecdote into measurement ( Figure 1 ) through three commitments. The first is to measure the whole lifecycle, through twenty-five tasks spanning all three stages. Ten Creation tasks commission a complete agent from a natural-language requirement, over workloads from GUI automation to formal theorem proving, each scoped against a mature open-source system; ten Editing tasks ask for new capabilities in the pinned codebase of a production agent such as OpenHands or Aider, capabilities that cut through crash recovery, permission fencing, and transactional state; and five Optimization tasks set a working agent against a measurable target. The second commitment is that evaluation should mirror the delegation it studies. Each task is framed as a commission: the builder sees only what a customer could provide, while acceptance also tests the stated clauses its examples leave out. The builder develops against the requirement and a few illustrative cases; held-out cases then test the frozen delivery in isolation. The third commitment follows from the absence of a prescribed reference implementation: every Creation and Editing execution is graded against a rubric derived from the requirement itself, and hard gates assign zero to invalid executions and inapplicable patches.\n\nWe put this instrument to work on five models as builders in the Codex harness, together with further pairings of harness and model. The verdict is sobering. The strongest builders average 19.1 on Creation and 31.6 on Editing, and in Optimization no builder closes more than 5 % of the remaining gap to the target on average, and only 13 of the 25 runs score above their starter. Creation runs that end development at 90 or above deliver agents averaging 9.9 on held-out cases. The gap between what a builder demonstrates and what it delivers is not the corner case of agent software engineering; it is the norm. The builders’ trajectories reveal recurring failures. Builders overfit to the visible development cases, and the delivery fails on the clauses those cases leave out; they ship hollow runtimes, code that reads as complete but does no work when run; and both builders and the agents they deliver claim completions that their own records contradict. Each of these modes names a habit that the next generation of coding agents can be built and trained to avoid. More development cases, a prescribed design, an instruction to test, and a longer case budget shift the scores but leave held-out Result low under every condition.\n\nIn sum, this paper makes three contributions. First, we formalize agent software engineering as a measurable problem, in which a builder must satisfy a requirement it can probe only through limited development feedback. Second, we deliver AgentSWE, twenty-five commissions whose acceptance executes the delivered agent on held-out cases and grades each run with a requirement-derived rubric or a native objective, and we evaluate various harness–model pairings against it. Third, we contribute an empirical analysis of the failure trajectories, which identifies recurring failure modes of coding agents and turns each into a direction for the next generation of coding agents.\n\n2 Related Work\n\nAgents that build and optimize agents.\n\nResearch on automated agent development has expanded both the scope of what can be designed and the stage at which adaptation occurs. Early approaches generate task-specific roles, tools, and collaboration patterns from task descriptions or seed agents ( Chen et al., 2024 ; Yuan et al., 2025 ; Tang et al., 2026 ) . Subsequent work formulates agent development as optimization, first over textual components such as instructions and demonstrations ( Yang et al., 2024a ; Khattab et al., 2023 ; Agrawal et al., 2025 ) , and then over broader design spaces containing agent modules, communication structures, and executable workflows ( Zhuge et al., 2024 ; Shang et al., 2025 ; Zhang et al., 2025c ; Zhang et al., 2025a ; Zhou et al., 2025 ) . This paradigm has further expanded to searching over complete code-defined agents and multi-agent scaffolds ( Hu et al., 2025 ; Rosser & Foerster, 2025 ) .\n\nA related line relaxes the assumption that the improvement procedure itself remains fixed. Initial systems support self-referential modification of an improver or agent logic ( Zelikman et al., 2023 ; Yin et al., 2025 ) , while later approaches expose complete implementations, harnesses, and meta-level improvement mechanisms to revision ( Robeyns et al., 2025 ; Zhang et al., 2025b ; Wang et al., 2025a ; Zhang et al., 2026b ; Lee et al., 2026 ; Hebbar et al., 2026 ) . Arbor ( Jin et al., 2026 ) carries hypotheses and evidence across long-horizon artifact optimization, whereas JIT-Agent ( Zhang et al., 2026a ) synthesizes task-conditioned executable harnesses at inference time. Together, these developments establish agent construction and optimization as a capability in its own right. AgentSWE makes this capability the object of evaluation, measuring whether general-purpose coding agents can create, modify, and optimize agents from heterogeneous user requirements.\n\nBenchmarks for agent construction.\n\nMost agent benchmarks hold the agent implementation fixed and evaluate its performance within a given environment. Recent benchmarks move beyond task completion by asking agents to construct or iteratively improve more complex artifacts. WorFBench ( Qiao et al., 2025 ) evaluates generated workflow structures, while benchmarks for machine-learning experimentation and research engineering evaluate improvement of executable research artifacts ( Huang et al., 2024 ; Nathani et al., 2025 ; Wijk et al., 2025 ) . AI4AI-Bench ( Chi et al., 2026 ) extends this setting to the redesign of model-training algorithms. Other evaluations isolate specific improvement mechanisms: SE-Bench ( Yuan et al., 2026 ) studies knowledge internalization, whereas OPT-BENCH ( Li et al., 2026 ) measures feedback-driven solution refinement. These benchmarks evaluate workflows, research artifacts, or optimized solutions rather than deployable agents.\n\nClosest to our setting, AIE-Bench ( Mishra et al., 2026 ) evaluates whether a meta-agent can improve a pinned target-agent scaffold under controlled mutation boundaries and trial budgets. AgentSWE extends this setting to the full agent-development lifecycle: creating agents from natural-language commissions, modifying existing agent codebases without regression and optimizing working agents toward measurable objectives. By freezing submissions and evaluating their behavior on held-out cases, AgentSWE measures whether coding agents can deliver the agents users actually request.\n\n3 Problem Formulation\n\nA commission prescribes no reference implementation, so we define the requirement, the delivered agent, the builder, and the fidelity of a delivery through observable behavior alone.\n\nA commission begins with a natural-language requirement q = ( g , ℐ , 𝒞 , ℛ ) q=(g,\\mathcal{I},\\mathcal{C},\\mathcal{R}) , which every AgentSWE task publishes in four parts: the goal g g , which states what the agent is for and what counts as serving its user; the interface contract ℐ \\mathcal{I} , which fixes how the agent is launched and which artifacts it must deliver; the constraints 𝒞 \\mathcal{C} , which bound what may be edited, installed, or invoked; and the resources ℛ \\mathcal{R} , such as data, repositories, and access to models and search. A case x x is one concrete instance of the commission. The requirement comes with a small set D D of public development cases, while the cases that matter, the ones the delivered agent will face in use, remain unwritten; we write 𝒳 q \\mathcal{X}_{q} for the space of cases the requirement implies.\n\nThe deliverable is itself an agent a = ( h , m ) a=(h,m) : a harness h h , the orchestration code that plans, routes, and retries ( Raj et al., 2026 ; Lee et al., 2026 ) , around a backbone model m m reached through a fixed gateway. Executing a a on a case x x in environment E E yields observable outcomes y ∼ a ⁡ ( x , E ) y\\sim a(x;E) . Because m m samples, the same delivery may satisfy a requirement on one execution and betray it on the next, so acceptance executes the agent and grades what it does.\n\nA coding agent, or builder , is an agent β \\beta whose actions are software engineering: reading a requirement, exploring and editing a workspace, running what it wrote, and deciding what to try next ( Yang et al., 2024b ; Wang et al., 2025b ) . Handed a commission, the builder produces an artifact a ^ ∼ β ⁡ ( q , a 0 , D , B ) \\hat{a}\\sim\\beta(q,a_{0},D;B) , where a 0 a_{0} is the initial artifact, possibly empty, D D the development cases it may probe freely, and B B a budget on time and compute.\n\n3.2 The Agent-for-Agent Problem\n\nThe customer cares about how faithfully a ^ \\hat{a} serves the requirement on cases it has not seen. Let μ q ​ ( x , y ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \\mu_{q}(x,y)\\in[0,1] be the graded satisfaction of requirement q q on case x x with outcome y y , and let V ⁡ ( a ^ ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } V(\\hat{a})\\in\\{0,1\\} be a validity gate on the non-negotiables of ℐ \\mathcal{I} and 𝒞 \\mathcal{C} : the agent launches, its deliverables parse, its changes respect the authorized surface. The fidelity of a delivered agent is\n\nGiven a requirement q q , an initial artifact a 0 a_{0} , development cases D D , and a budget B B , a builder β \\beta must deliver an agent a ^ ∼ β ⁡ ( q , a 0 , D , B ) \\hat{a}\\sim\\beta(q,a_{0},D;B) maximizing fidelity F ⁡ ( a ^ , q ) F(\\hat{a};q) .\n\nWe estimate fidelity on a held-out set D ¯ ⊂ 𝒳 q \\bar{D}\\subset\\mathcal{X}_{q} , disjoint from D D and designed to exercise the promises in g g , graded by a rubric that realizes μ q \\mu_{q} . Writing F D F_{D} and F D ¯ F_{\\bar{D}} for the estimates on the development and held-out cases, the gap between demonstrated and delivered fidelity, F D − F D ¯ F_{D}-F_{\\bar{D}} , measures how much better the delivered agent does on the cases the builder saw than on the cases it did not.\n\n3.3 Task Families\n\nThe three lifecycle stages are instances of Definition 3.1 that differ in the initial artifact a 0 a_{0} and in the shape of μ q \\mu_{q} .\n\nCreation ( a 0 = ∅ a_{0}=\\emptyset ).\n\nThe builder starts from an empty workspace and must deliver a complete agent under the interface contract ℐ \\mathcal{I} ; fidelity is estimated on held-out cases as in Equation 1 .\n\nEditing ( a 0 = a_{0}= a production system).\n\nThe initial artifact is the pinned codebase of a working agent product, and the requirement commissions new capabilities. The constraints 𝒞 \\mathcal{C} confine the builder’s changes to an authorized region, and satisfaction has two parts,\n\nthe commissioned behavior μ new \\mu_{\\mathrm{new}} and the preservation μ reg \\mu_{\\mathrm{reg}} of everything the product already did, checked by its public suite and published contracts ( Jimenez et al., 2024 ) .\n\nOptimization ( a 0 = a_{0}= a working agent).\n\nThe initial artifact already satisfies the interface, and the requirement names a measurable objective J J on a",

    "version_id": "v-9bde3907428df8ef68a78026",

    "title": "The Work Behind Delegation: A Framework for Supervising AI Coding Agents",
    "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2609.24234",
    "published_at": "2026-09-21T07:57:48Z",
    "text": "The Work Behind Delegation: A Framework for Supervising AI Coding Agents\n\nAs AI coding agents carry out development tasks with greater autonomy, developers are shifting from direct implementation toward supervising delegated work. Yet existing research offers limited understanding of how developers organize supervisory activities into connected workflows. Drawing on observations and workflow diagrams from 19 experienced developers, we reconfigure Sheridan’s framework of human supervisory control into seven stages and the connecting loops for supervising AI coding agents. We applied the framework to public developer discussions on Reddit and found that supervisory demands extend across stages and that developers manage them by concentrating effort in planning, delegating supervisory work to other agents, and turning recurring guidance into reusable assets. Our framework provides a useful analytical lens for understanding how developers supervise AI coding agents by capturing how supervision is structured in agentic software development.\n\nAI coding tools have rapidly expanded their capabilities, moving from assisting developers ( Santos et al., 2025 ) to carrying out software development tasks with greater autonomy ( Mozannar et al., 2024 ; Robbes et al., 2026 ) . With the emergence of AI coding agents such as Claude Code ( Anthropic, 2025 ) and Codex ( OpenAI, 2025b ) , developers can now provide high-level direction and leave the execution to the agent. Given a task description, agents can autonomously plan their own approach, explore the codebase, run commands, and make changes across multiple files ( Dong et al., 2025 ; Guo et al., 2025 ) . As developers delegate more of this execution to agents, their role shifts from direct implementation toward coordination and oversight ( Alenezi, 2026 ) . Therefore, developers increasingly act as supervisors of AI coding agents, setting the direction of delegated work, tracking progress, and evaluating its output ( Sapkota et al., 2025 ) .\n\nAs developers take on this supervisory role, understanding what effective supervision of AI coding agents requires becomes important. Prior work has examined what developers do at particular points in the process, such as providing clear context and explicit instructions ( Huang et al., 2025 ) , asking agents to explain their changes ( Baumann et al., 2026 ) , and providing corrective feedback when execution goes wrong ( Chen et al., 2026b ) . However, supervision extends across a development task, and effort invested at one point can affect what needs to be monitored, corrected, or reviewed at subsequent points in the task ( Dhanorkar et al., 2026 ; Zhou et al., 2026 ; Chen et al., 2026a ) . Given limited human attention and effort, these dependencies highlight the need to consider supervision as a connected process rather than as a set of separate activities. Understanding this process requires an analytical approach that captures how supervisory activities are organized and connected across a task.\n\nEarlier research on automation provides a useful foundation for understanding how human roles change as task execution is increasingly automated by systems such as aircraft autopilots and industrial robots ( Parasuraman et al., 2000 ; Sheridan, 2012 ) . Among this body of work, we build on Sheridan’s framework of human supervisory control , which conceptualizes supervision as a process organized around five stages: Plan , Teach , Monitor , Intervene , and Learn ( Sheridan, 1976 ; Sheridan, 1992 ) . These stages are linked through recurrent loops, highlighting how one stage shapes subsequent supervisory demands. While Sheridan’s framework was grounded largely in automated systems that executed human-specified goals or instructions, AI coding agents have greater autonomy in determining how to accomplish delegated tasks. Hence, it remains unclear whether Sheridan’s supervisory framework can fully capture supervision under this more agentic form of delegation.\n\nThis motivates a central question for our work: How do developers organize supervision over the course of a task delegated to AI coding agents? To answer this, we developed a framework for supervising AI coding agents by reconfiguring Sheridan’s framework (Figure 1 ). The framework was based on a qualitative study with 19 experienced developers, where we observed participants working with agents and asked them to reconstruct their supervision workflows as diagrams. Our analysis identified seven supervisory stages— Plan , Monitor , Wait , Review , Teach , Manual Fix , and Update Assets —and loops connecting them. Together, these stages and loops capture how supervision is organized in agentic software development, providing an analytic lens for studying developer supervision of AI coding agents.\n\nWe then applied the framework to developer discussions on Reddit to examine supervisory patterns across the development process. We identified three demands that span multiple stages: maintaining alignment between agent behavior and developer intent, enforcing quality beyond task completion, and maintaining understanding of agents’ work. We also found three strategies for managing these demands: concentrating effort in planning, distributing supervisory work to other agents, and externalizing repeated supervision into reusable assets. Through the framework, we show how supervisory demands extend across stages and how developers redistribute their effort in response.\n\nThe contributions of this paper are as follows:\n\n• A framework for supervising AI coding agents that consists of seven stages and the loops connecting them.\n\n• Findings from applying the framework to examine supervisory demands and the strategies developers use to manage them across the development process.\n\n2. Related Work\n\nWe review prior work to contextualize our framework for developer supervision of AI coding agents: (1) AI-assisted software development, (2) frameworks for human–agent collaboration, and (3) human roles in the agentic era.\n\n2.1. AI-Assisted Software Development\n\nAI assistance in software development has shifted from providing localized code suggestions ( Sarkar et al., 2022 ; Prather et al., 2023 ; Fan et al., 2026 ; Ma et al., 2023 ) toward increasingly autonomous AI agents that can carry out development tasks with limited human intervention ( Liu et al., 2026a ; Fawzy et al., 2026 ; Ray, 2025 ) . As AI systems have become more capable ( Dong et al., 2025 ; Feng et al., 2025 ) , how developers interact with them has also changed.\n\nPrior work has examined AI assistance in software development and its integration into developer workflows. Early studies focused on developers’ interactions with individual AI-generated suggestions, examining how they evaluated, accepted, and modified suggestions during programming ( Barke et al., 2023 ; Mozannar et al., 2024 ) . More recent work has expanded attention to AI use across programming sessions and broader development workflows, drawing on interviews with practitioners ( Ullrich et al., 2025 ; Dhanorkar et al., 2026 ) , interaction logs from real-world use ( Wu et al., 2026 ; Tang et al., 2026 ) , and longitudinal telemetry ( Sergeyuk et al., 2026 ) . Together, these studies highlight the importance of understanding developer–AI interaction as AI becomes deeply integrated into software development.\n\nAgentic AI introduces new dynamics of developer–AI interaction. AI agents can proactively plan and execute multi-step actions ( Chen et al., 2026b ) , incorporate broader contextual information ( Guo et al., 2025 ) , and produce artifacts across development workflows ( Zamfirescu-Pereira et al., 2025 ) . As developers delegate more execution to agents, supervision becomes increasingly important, yet its organization across a development task remains underexplored. Therefore, in this work, we examine the supervisory activities developers perform and how these activities connect throughout the process.\n\n2.2. Frameworks for Human–Agent Collaboration\n\nA growing body of empirical work has examined human–agent collaboration through interviews ( Dhanorkar et al., 2026 ) , observational studies ( Huang et al., 2025 ; Shome et al., 2026 ) , and real-world interaction logs ( Tang et al., 2026 ; Baumann et al., 2026 ) . Building on these findings, researchers have developed different conceptualizations of human–agent interaction, capturing recurring modes, activities, and dimensions of collaboration. For example, Barke et al. (2023) distinguish between exploration and acceleration modes of interaction with programming assistants, while Dhanorkar et al. (2026) identify four forms of oversight: a priori control, co-planning, real-time monitoring, and post-hoc review. Other studies describe human–agent interaction in terms of the degree of human involvement ( Chen et al., 2026b ) , time allocation across activity states ( Mozannar et al., 2024 ) , and the distribution of initiative between human and system ( Maier et al., 2026 ) .\n\nBeyond these study-specific conceptualizations, recent work has proposed more explicit frameworks for human–agent collaboration. Feng et al. (2025) structure collaboration around levels of agent autonomy and corresponding user roles, while Wang and Lu (2025) propose a process-oriented framework that connects interaction, process, and infrastructure. These frameworks provide systematic ways to structure human–agent collaboration, but offer limited guidance to collaboration centered on supervising delegated agent work. In this work, we develop a framework that captures the supervisory activities involved and how they connect across a development task.\n\n2.3. Human Roles in the Agentic Era\n\nThe emergence of AI as a collaborator is not unique to software development. Similar shifts toward human–AI collaboration have been observed across domains such as design ( Xu et al., 2025 ; Cavallin and Spagnol, 2026 ) , writing ( Lehmann et al., 2026 ) , and knowledge work ( Yun et al., 2025 ) , where AI systems have become integrated into everyday workflows. As these systems become more agentic and take on greater responsibility for execution, human roles are being reconceptualized. Rather than acting as primary executors while AI provides passive assistance, humans are increasingly shifting toward supervisory roles over delegated work by defining goals ( Feng et al., 2026b ; Ullrich et al., 2025 ; Ma et al., 2025 ) , evaluating outcomes ( Feng et al., 2026a ) , and intervening when necessary ( Zhou et al., 2026 ) .\n\nThese shifts also align with longstanding perspectives in automation research, which describe how responsibilities shift between humans and systems as system autonomy increases ( Parasuraman et al., 2000 ; Sheridan, 2012 ) . Taken together, these perspectives point toward a shift in human contribution from direct execution toward higher-level activities such as providing context, exercising judgment, and guiding AI-generated work ( Feng et al., 2026a ; Cheruvu, 2025 ) . Building on these observations, we examine how this shift manifests in software development as developers increasingly supervise work delegated to AI agents.\n\nIn this section, we provide background on Sheridan’s human supervisory control framework, which serves as the theoretical starting point for our work examining how developers supervise AI coding agents. The human supervisory control framework describes how a human supervisor directs and oversees automated systems such as aircraft autopilots and industrial robots ( Sheridan, 1992 ; Sheridan, 1976 ; Sheridan, 2012 ) . The framework consists of five supervisory stages: Plan , Teach , Monitor , Intervene , and Learn , connected by recurring loops as illustrated in Figure 2 . We briefly explain each stage below.\n\nThe supervisor begins with Plan , where they develop an understanding of the process and system capabilities, then determine the goals, constraints, and overall strategy. In Teach , these plans are translated into instructions for the automated system. During execution, Monitor involves observing the system and assessing whether it is progressing as intended, while Intervene occurs when the supervisor needs to revise the instructions or take more direct control. Revising instructions returns the process to Teach , whereas satisfactory completion leads to Learn , where the supervisor reflects on key events and prior decisions to inform future Plan activities. Together, these stages form recurring control loops in which supervision can return to earlier stages as execution progresses (Figure 2 ).\n\nWe conducted a qualitative study with experienced software developers to develop a framework for supervising AI coding agents. We used Sheridan’s framework of human supervisory control ( Sheridan, 1976 ; Sheridan, 1992 ) (§ 3 ) as a theoretical starting point to examine how its stages and loops translate to the supervision of AI coding agents as developers delegate more implementation to them. We observed participants working on their own coding tasks with their usual agent setups and asked them to reconstruct their supervision workflows as diagrams. We analyzed these workflow diagrams together with task observations and think-aloud data to develop our framework, which we introduce in § 5 .\n\nIn this section, we describe how we designed and conducted the study in detail. The study was reviewed and approved by the Institutional Review Board (IRB) of our institution prior to conducting the study.\n\nWe distributed a screening survey through university forums and developer communities in South Korea, as well as LinkedIn groups and networks for international recruitment. The survey collected information about respondents’ software development background, AI coding agent use (i.e., which agents they used, how frequently they used them, and the types of tasks they delegated), and the coding task they could bring to the study session.\n\nBased on the survey responses, we applied three eligibility criteria: (1) at least three years of professional software development or graduate-level research experience in a software-intensive field (e.g., computer science, electrical engineering), (2) regular use of AI coding agents for substantive development tasks in professional, research, or other real-world projects, and (3) the ability to bring a shareable coding task with a clearly defined goal for a 40-minute session. These criteria ensured that participants had sufficient development experience to judge agent output critically and sufficient familiarity with agents to have established supervisory practices of their own.\n\nWe recruited 20 participants who met these criteria. One participant was unable to use their regular agent setup during the session and was excluded from the analysis, resulting in a final sample of 19 participants. The full list of participants and their information is shown in Table 1 . We deliberately recruited participants across diverse roles and development backgrounds to capture variation in how developers supervise AI coding agents.\n\nSessions were conducted in Korean or English, and Korean transcripts were translated into English for analysis. All eligible participants received either a 120 USD Amazon gift card or 150,000 KRW ( ≈ \\approx 98 USD) for a 90-minute study.\n\nFollowing Huang et al. (2025) , participants were asked to bring their own task from their ongoing project rather than complete a given standardized task. This allowed us to observe realistic development workflows ( Becker et al., 2025 ) , while introducing natural variation in tasks, codebases, and agent configurations across the study sessions ( Ko et al., 2015 ) .\n\nEach task needed a clearly defined goal that could be pursued within approximately 40 minutes. This scope allowed us to observe supervision end-to-end within a single session. Tasks could come from ongoing work, open-source projects, side projects, or research code, as long as they could be",

    "text": "Subscribe for AI news & product updates\n\nIntroducing Agentic Batch Changes: the frontier agent for code change at scale\n\nCodebase-wide changes across hundreds or thousands of repositories can now be run by one engineer.\n\nEvery large codebase has a list. The .NET upgrade that touches ten thousand repositories. The deprecated API that a dozen services still call. The CI config that was standardized years ago and has drifted in every direction since. Each one is manageable in a single repository. Across the whole codebase, the repositories are rarely identical, so the fix that works in one place breaks in the next, and there is no cost-effective way to roll the change out exhaustively.\n\nThe list grows, and the bigger the change, the less anyone can vouch that it reached everywhere it needed to. One migration turns into hundreds of pull requests, and you still can't confidently say whether the work is done.\n\nWe have been working on this problem for years. Batch Changes, our engine for coordinated change across a codebase, already runs these rollouts for some of the largest engineering organizations in the world. You program the change, it opens pull requests everywhere the change applies, and then tracks them until they merge. Customers have merged hundreds of thousands of changesets this way, tens of millions of lines of code. The largest single Batch Change we have seen merged more than 2,200 changesets from one change.\n\nThe pressure is different now, and only getting worse. Coding agents write code faster than any team can read it. Nobody holds the whole codebase in their head anymore, so every change is a bet. The same agents that make one repository easy to change have made the codebase as a whole harder to maintain, and the list keeps getting longer.\n\nAgentic Batch Changes is available today, built on technology designed for the world's largest codebases.\n\nDescribe the change once. The agent takes it from there.\n\nAgentic Batch Changes is built on Batch Changes and Deep Search, our agent for understanding complex codebases. Together they form an agent harness specialized for applying changes across codebases at any scale, from thousands of repositories to the largest monorepos.\n\nYou tell it what needs to change, in plain language. It scopes the work across your indexed codebase, builds a plan, tries the change in one repository, then rolls it out. Where repositories differ, it adapts. A diff is not a shipped change, so when CI fails, it reacts. It keeps working until the pull requests are ready for a human to review and merge, and it tracks merge status the whole way so you can see the change land from one place.\n\nAnything a single coding agent can do in one repository, Agentic Batch Changes can orchestrate across all of your repositories at the same time.\n\nAgentic Batch Changes writes scripts more often than it deploys coding agents, which is what keeps it efficient at scale\n\nFor each piece of a plan, Agentic Batch Changes decides whether the change needs judgment or just needs doing. When it needs judgment, it hands the repository to a coding agent with specific instructions and codebase context. Most of the time it writes a script, because a large migration is mostly the same change made over and over, and running a script is far more efficient than working out the same change a hundred times over. You see diffs as they are generated, you can step in at any point, and when the agent needs a decision, it stops and asks.\n\nSuccess we saw from the Beta\n\nDuring the Beta, customers merged nearly a thousand changesets created by Agentic Batch Changes, on work that ranged from security remediation to library migrations.\n\nMercari, a Japan-based marketplace, turned to Agentic Batch Changes for a security fix across many repositories. Patrick Klitzke, Team Lead at Mercari, used it to identify a GitHub Actions environment-variable injection vulnerability across Mercari's codebase and began patching it across the affected repositories.\n\n\"I looked at a GitHub injection issue where you have to set environment variables correctly. I was able to fix it with one prompt on both the Help Center frontend and backend, then extended this to all repos in Mercari. I found around 80 potential repos affected.\" \"With the help of Agentic Batch Changes, you're able to handle repos that have similar, but not identical setups. A normal scripted change would most likely be a text search and replace operation without any context of how it's actually used.\" Patrick Klitzke, Team Lead at Mercari\n\nAt Canva, the use case was a library migration. The alternative would have been a handful of oversized pull requests, or a spreadsheet to keep track of many small ones. Instead, Agentic Batch Changes raised the pull requests and tracked each one to merge from a single view.\n\n\"We used Agentic Batch Changes to raise and merge 50+ pull requests across our repos as part of a library migration. The web UI made it easy to track each PR and its status. Without it, we would have been managing either huge PRs or spreadsheets; instead, Agentic Batch Changes made the process easier for both reviewers and me.\" William L., Senior Software Engineer, Canva\n\nSecurity remediation and library migrations are two of the use cases. We've also seen dependency upgrades, deprecated API replacement, framework rollouts, CI pipeline modernization, and end-of-life deadlines. These are starting points, not a fixed list. Customers keep finding uses we didn't plan for, and we want to hear about them.\n\nOnly pay for what merges\n\nWith the launch of Agentic Batch Changes, we're rolling out outcome-based pricing. You pay per changeset merged into your codebase, not per token, seat, or attempt. If it opens a pull request and your team decides not to merge it, you don't pay for it. We priced it this way because it's the clearest way to say we're confident in the work, and because it lets you justify spend against something concrete: a change that landed rather than a forecast of compute usage.\n\nAgentic Batch Changes is available today for Sourcegraph Cloud customers, on by default.\n\nTalk to your account team or schedule a demo , and learn more at sourcegraph.com/agentic-batch-changes .\n\nUnblock your organization. InfoQ Homepage Podcasts What Does It Really Mean To Program With AI: A Conversation with Joy Ebertz What Does\nIt Really Mean To Program With AI: A Conversation with Joy Ebertz Oct 12, 2026 In this podcast, Michael Stiefel spoke to Joy Ebertz about how the use of artificial intelligence is changing the way architects and developers do their jobs. Focusing on the long\nterm success of the business is one of the most neglected tasks of an architect. This task becomes particularly important as the nature of artificial intelligence changes the landscape of software development. Architects must be especially aware of requirements and their inherent ambiguity. More than\never, they must be expert at explaining technology to the business people, and the business to the technology people. On the flip side, goals must be clearly explained to AI agents, and tests must be unambiguously written. Industry practice is uncertain on how to incorporate\nchanges due to bug reports or new requirements. Should it be done incrementally, or should the module’s or application’s code base be regenerated? This is especially challenging in existing systems, especially old ones where nobody knows what the real requirements were. Unambiguous requirements definition may\nturn out to be the gating factor in AI driven software development because code reviews seem not to be effective, and verification of the AI work is a great burden. The conversation then shifted to talk about the future of software developers. The industry needs\nto find a way to use AI so that human understanding of how software systems work does not atrophy. The flexibility that AI coding gives you reinforces the idea that constraints, and the recognition of the assumptions you are making, are critical concepts on which\nfuture developers need to focus. If you look at how AI is actually used, it seems to boil down to two use cases - vibe coding for things that do not have to be perfect such as many internal tools, and essential systems where you\nspecify to the AI, and validate the results very carefully. In the latter scenario, you are effectively pair programming with the AI. Here those constraints and assumptions must be understood. In addition, the industry has not figured out a way to train future software engineers\nsince AI is doing the tasks on which junior engineers were traditionally trained. One of the important, often neglected parts of an architect’s job is to focus on the long term implications of technical decisions that might make or break a business’ survival. It is\nstill not certain whether AI software development generally lends itself more naturally to microservices or monoliths. What is meant by coding with artificial intelligence has a wide range of meaning. It ranges from vibe coding, developing internal tools, treating the AI as a closely supervised\npair programmer, to being similar to a constrained senior developer. As artificial intelligence takes on more of the coding workload, precise requirements and comprehensive tests in order to remove ambiguity becomes critical. Industry has not yet figured out how to train junior engineers as AI\ncoding now does much of the work on which they were trained. Michael Stiefel : Welcome to The Architect's Podcast where we discuss what it means to be an architect and how architects actually do their job. Today's guest is Joy Ebertz, who is a\nprincipal software engineer at Imprint where she is currently trying to detangle their configuration. She's primarily a backend focused developer with extensive experience in microservice architecture, authorization frameworks, creating REST API standards, feature flagging, and more. Prior to Imprint, she worked at Harness, Split, and\nBox in staff plus roles. In addition to designing software and writing a lot of code with the help of AI, she also maintains a blog with both career and technical topics. In her free time, she does a lot of traveling, reading, and running\nridiculously long distances, mostly on trails. It's great to have you here on the podcast. And I'd like to start out by asking you, were you trained as an architect? How did you become an architect? What is an Architect? [ 01:09 ] Joy Ebertz\n: Yes. Well, thanks for having me, first of all. I guess like most career paths, it was a bit meandering. I mean, if you had asked me pretty soon out of college, \"Where do you want to go eventually?\" I probably would've said something\nlike architect. I thought something like that was pretty cool. I was generally drawn to that. But then over time, I tried a few different things. When I was at Box, I got pushed into management at one point. I lasted about a year and\na half and decided I absolutely hated it. So that was the end of that career progression. I moved back to the IC track. And from there, it wasn't quite ... We didn't have a role called architect at Box, but over time, as I\ngot more senior and started picking up more responsibilities, I tended to gravitate towards the more overarching type roles, the roles that looked into a lot of different spaces and how things fit together, how things generally worked. And then at some point we started\na project, actually we started this project five times, but on one of the iterations, we were trying to break our monolith into microservices. And so as a part of that, we spun up a small team of individuals working on doing sort of a\ndomain-driven design exploration of the entire space. And I was lucky enough to be chosen as one of the people on that team, which caused me to learn a lot about some of the more theoretical side of architecture and dig in and really think\nabout how code fits together. And from there, I would say I just sort of tended in that direction. And to be honest, I've never had an official architect title, but if you ask me to place myself, that's generally where I would place myself\nin my roles. Michael Stiefel : Well, a lot of people who function as architects don't have that title. In fact, if you remember, there was a time in a certain phase of Agile where they said, \"We're not going to have any architects\". And\nthe idea was that architecture would somehow magically emerge and everybody would be an architect. And to my way of thinking, that always doesn't work because if no one has responsibility for it, it usually doesn't get done. And I don't know if that's your\nexperience. Joy Ebertz : Yes. Michael Stiefel : The way I like to think about architecture, and tell me whether you disagree with that, is that the architect is responsible for those things that cannot be written in a use case. The ilities, the security,\nscalability, the whole user experience. I'm not talking about the user interface, but the user experience. And that really is where the architect role shines. Joy Ebertz : I would say some of that, but also thinking long-term. I feel like sometimes some of the\nthings you mentioned, the team might think about anyway because they happen to be in the RFC template or what have you. But I've also seen a lot of times people will think a little bit too small, either in terms of timeline or in\nterms of only thinking about their team or what have you. And part of where I see the architect shine is pulling in those, \"Where are we going next and how does this fit with that?\" as well as, \"This other team is doing this\nthing, how does your thing work well with that?\" And kind of pulling the big picture into what's actually being built. Michael Stiefel : And part of that, as I remember from your QCon talk, I mean, you were talking at the time about technical\ndebt, but the technical debt is just an example of this. You have to trade off, do we sacrifice too much for the long term? How much does it really matter? What's the business case for doing that? So it's really, from what you're saying,\nit's not only thinking in the long term in terms of technical things, but in terms of understanding the business and explaining that to the business people as well. I mean, you could build something absolutely technically perfect that's going\nto last a hundred billion customers, whatever, but if your business doesn't survive to next month, it doesn't matter. Michael Stiefel : Right. I think that's a valid point. So if you have a monolith or you have one repo or you have something large, it\nmay be harder for the AI to make the micro changes or the larger changes that have to be made if you're dealing with a monolith rather than a microservice. Because I had someone on the podcast who we talked about this and his whole\nidea was you have the AI generate the microservice, and when you get a bug report in, you just regenerate the microservice from scratch because you can do that with an AI much more easily than you can with a human.     "title": "How Will We Train Developers If AI Does the Routine Work: A Conversation with Scott Hanselman",
