    "text": "InfoQ Software Architects' Newsletter A monthly overview of things you need to know as an architect or aspiring architect. View an example We protect your privacy. InfoQ Homepage Podcasts How Will We Train Developers if AI Does the Routine Work? A Conversation with Scott Hanselman\nHow Will We Train Developers if AI Does the Routine Work? A Conversation with Scott Hanselman Sep 14, 2026 In this podcast, Michael Stiefel spoke to Scott Hanselman about developing new software engineers when artificial intelligence agents are doing most of the work on which\njunior developers were trained. Hanselman suggests the software industry should adopt a preceptorship model similar to the nursing profession. Under this model, the industry needs preceptors whose sole job is to train new engineers, and these preceptors would be evaluated on how well they train\npeople rather than how much code they ship. The use of AI agents requires experienced engineers because while they are capable of generating software features, they perform poorly at software architecture because they often miss the larger context. In addition, engineers must maintain oversight of\nartificial intelligence tools by acting as reviewers and coordinators to ensure that human judgment remains central to the development process. The conversation then broadened to discuss the environment in which agentic AI should be used. When businesses prioritize short term, hyper-optimization, they neglect the longer\nterm investments in human capital and mentorship which will make it impossible to cultivate future senior talent. Beyond that, Hanselman addressed the sociological impacts of modern development, observing that the intersection of remote work, social media, and artificial intelligence severely isolates developers. To counter this\nisolation and ensure healthy growth, intentional human connection and in-person interactions are vital for the next generation of users and developers of technology. The software industry should adopt a preceptorship model for developing software engineers. With AI taking over the tasks on which junior engineers\nwere traditionally trained, industry needs preceptors, whose sole job is to train new engineers. This is a model that has worked quite well in the nursing profession. Preceptors are evaluated on how well they train engineers, not how much code they have shipped. AI is\nhighly capable of generating software features but performs poorly at designing overarching software architecture, often missing the big picture. Engineers must maintain oversight of AI tools by acting as reviewers and coordinators, ensuring that human judgment remains central to the development process. Businesses need to\nprioritize long-term investments in human capital and mentorship over short-term hyper-optimization to prevent the collapse of the junior engineer pipeline. The intersection of remote work, social media, and AI isolates developers, making intentional human connection and in-person interaction vital for their healthy growth. Sponsored by\nTiger Data Analytical queries killing your Postgres performance? Tiger Data (creators of TimescaleDB) extends Postgres with hybrid storage, 95% compression, and continuous aggregates — one system, petabyte scale, no re-architecting. Cloudflare, Schneider, Axpo, and more already run on it. Just Postgres, managed for you. Try\nit free at tigerdata.com/go/trial . Michael Stiefel : Welcome to the Architects Podcast, where we discuss what it means to be an architect and how architects actually do their job. We are very lucky today to have as our guest, Scott Hanselman, who is a\nprogrammer, teacher, and speaker. He works out of his home office in Portland, Oregon for Microsoft GitHub as a vice president and member of the technical staff. He works on open source and agentic coding tools like GitHub, Copilot, CLI, along with the Windows development\nplatform. Before Microsoft, he was the chief architect at Corillian Corporation, now a part of CheckFree for six plus years. Previously, he worked as a principal consultant at Step Technology, speaking, writing, consulting, and very much not getting rich during Web 1.0, although he worked\non a bunch of websites you probably have heard of. He also worked at Intel, Nike, and consulted all over the world. He was an adjunct professor at OIT teaching C and Web Services and created the first Palm Pilot diabetes management system in 1998\nand sold it to a healthcare company some years later. It's great to have you here on the podcast. How Did You Become An Architect? [ 01:30 ] And I'd like to start out by asking you, were you trained as an architect? How did\nyou become an architect? It's not something you woke up and decided, today I'm going to be an architect. Scott Hanselman : I mean, architect is one of those titles that one could probably just give themselves like CTO. You'll meet a CTO at somewhere\nlike a big Fortune 5000 company or a Fortune 500 company and they're an architect or they're a CTO and then you'll meet someone who's at Panera with their buddy and they're a founder and they're a CTO as well. So, these are titles that\nare kind of made up, but I am trained in software engineering. I do point out interestingly enough that I don't have a computer science degree. I have a software engineering degree, which is the practice of shipping, which is different than compiler theory, computer\ntheory, and the things that one learns in computer science many, many, many years ago. I helped write some of the tests for the Microsoft certified software architect stuff with Clemens Vasters lo these 20, 25 years ago. But what is an architect? Is it\nsomeone that knows UML or knows how to do Visio? Are they the one in the room with good taste? Are they the software sommelier? I don't know. Michael Stiefel : Maybe with AI, we'll get the PowerPoint compiler and that will be- AI is\nNot A Good Software Architect [ 02:49 ] Scott Hanselman : We definitely have the PowerPoint compiler. It compiles crap though. But I have said recently on podcasts that AIs generate features extremely well. They do not generate architecture very well. They tend to be\nsomewhat myopic in their view and they definitely miss the big picture. And I'm currently working through a problem with an application that I'm working on where the AI has over the last several weeks created a god object. Just a super struct that I\nwould not have created, but it's kind of a cancer that's building inside the application and I need to get it out of there before it collapses under its own weight. So, the AI did a very poor job of designing an architecture. How Will\nthe Next Generation of Software Engineers Be Trained? [ 03:32 ] Michael Stiefel : That's an interesting segue into something I know we want to talk about is that you and Mark Russinovich wrote an opinion piece in the Communications of the ACM last April\non redefining the software engineering profession for AI. And in it, you discuss the difficulty of training new software engineers when AI is doing all the work we traditionally trained entry-level software engineers. Can you explain what you meant by this and the culture of\nbeing a preceptor at scale? The Preceptor Model For Training Software Engineers [ 04:04 ] Scott Hanselman : So, the idea is in any internship, apprenticeship, master student, teacher student scenario, often the student is doing the wax on, wax off work. They're doing the\ntoil, they're doing the tedium. And it is through the “wax the car” that you learn karate. And if that job is now done by AI, then at what altitude, as I mix my metaphors, does an early in career person enter? Do they just\nskip the boring bits? Is it important to do the pushups and pull-ups and stuff? And I think that it is important. I think that we are skipping the easy bits and it is extremely dopamine positive and very exciting. And it's certainly a thrill.\nI'm watching my early-in-career and non-technical coworkers get very, very excited creating software and vibing stuff. But without trying to gatekeep the work, I don't believe that you can vibe into production, or at least not yet. The Role of a Preceptor is to Create\nEngineers [ 05:15 ] I feel like early in-career, people need guidance. They need a mentor. But the term mentor or apprentice, journeyman, these are kind of outdated. So, Mark and I looked towards successful industries that are regulated and managed and create successful seniors.\nAnd we found that in nursing. My wife is a nurse and she is a nurse preceptor. And a preceptor is an interesting word that is almost exclusively currently used in medicine to refer to the teaching nurse. And that job is an explicit and\nspecific job. Not every nurse is a nurse preceptor. And by having that level of formality, such that the nurse preceptor, the nurse trainer is metriced and their job performance is measured on whether or not they can make nurses. They can mint new senior\nnurses. In software though, like if I joined your team and you're the CTO or the architect, and you're the boss and you make me the intern or the apprentice, upon entrance, I'm less than. And that I have to dig myself out of the\nhole that you've put me in. And then if I don't hack it, you'll go out. Hanselman couldn't hack it. We'll grind them up and spit them out and we'll get another one. So, then interns become a dime a dozen. And if they can't\ncrawl their way out of the bucket, then go and find more. But with preceptors, their job is to make more nurses. So, maybe if we couldn't make more nurses, it's on the trainer. Their annual review would be like, \"Maybe Michael shouldn't be a\ntrainer. Maybe he needs some more training to be a trainer\". So, there's a formality to it. So, instead of a tech lead with a bunch of cleaves working for them, you have a tech lead working alongside a preceptor trainer. And that trainer's job\nis not to ship code, but to ship engineers. So, like after this podcast, I'm going to have my scrum and we're going to cut a release of the product that I'm working on. And there'll be a very mixed group of young people and\nlater in career people. And we're going to do some pair programming and we're going to do some fellowship and some working on stuff together to make sure that we are not just shipping the thing. We're going to cut a release at noon, but\nthat we are shipping senior engineers. That's a big long answer. A lot of rambling there. But I think you understand that the idea is - focus on making people better, not just shipping more crap. The New Role of the University [ 07:57 ]\nMichael Stiefel : So, two questions come to mind right away when you say that. One is what then becomes the role of software engineering education in the universities if this is the preceptor? And also, does remote work contribute to the problem and the\nlack of mentorship, or does it actually help? Scott Hanselman : As someone who has worked remotely now for many years, my entire career at Microsoft has been remote. I would say that that is hard for anyone and it's even harder for an early\nin career. So, I would argue that remote work is not helping grow people. Particularly like I've got young men in my house, 18 and 20, and they're going to go out into the world. There is value in them shaking hands, looking people in\nthe eye, talking to human beings. And I know that this might sound like two old men on a podcast shaking their fist at the cloud, but human beings are meant to hang out together. We are meant to be social. I got a lot\nof value sitting at whiteboards with senior engineers, having them explain to me how things work. If you are an online person who does their socializing online, does their video gaming online, does their everything online, as they're dating online, and now your work is\nonline, then that shows a fundamental collapse of the third place. The Collapse of the Third Place [ 09:27 ] Work, home, and the third place. And if there is nowhere, if work, home and the third place are all the same place. I don't\nknow how you can grow into a fully formed and healthy individual. So, yes, remote work is a challenge. We can push through it, but it requires a lot of metacognition and a lot of focus on listening to your body, listening to yourself, making\nsure that you are a whole person. I do drive up to Seattle every four to six weeks to make sure that my badge still works and that the young people are still present. Although I do have folks that are remote in other places.\nIt's the perfect storm of COVID, remote work, social media addiction and AI that threatens to break the young people. And I can only focus on one of those. Michael Stiefel : So, I want to get back to university in a minute, but this\nis interesting. What you're saying requires acknowledgement on both sides of the equation because it's the young people, as you say, who live in this virtual world that have to learn the importance of the in-person. And you also need the companies to understand that\nin order to be profitable in the long term, they have to be willing to have these preceptors to help people along, in the office, if possible. Measuring How Engineers Thrive [ 10:51 ] Scott Hanselman : Yes. So, you have to acknowledge that hitting\nthe ground running is not a thing and that an early in career person fresh out of school is not going to start slinging code within a couple of days. We do have a metric in our group and we call it engineering thrive, where\nwe call it time to first PR. So, here's your laptop. Welcome to the company. It's Monday. And it's not a productivity metric. It's not, \"Oh, Michael joined the company on Monday and he got a PR landed on Wednesday or Thursday\". That's actually a\nmorale metric. Think about how exciting it will be for them to land their first PR and how enabled they will be. So, when we talk about engineering thrive, we talk about how do they feel in their personal growth? Even if it's a one\nline change, it shows that they can begin the engineering loop. So, back to your question I didn't answer about how this fits into the relationship with universities. They leave their parents' house, they go to university, and then they land a job. And if\nthat entire experience is entirely online, that doesn't facilitate their growth. And if they're using AI at every step, there's also a lack of growth. So, we're trying to insert specific conscious growth moments at every step. And in doing that, we're going to have\nto coordinate with universities to make sure that AI ethics, AI internals, how AI is used, how AI should be used, so that they don't externalize their brains entirely and have them atrophy just like their social skills have atrophied. The Danger For Engineers of\nAnthromorphizing AI [ 12:32 ] Michael Stiefel : I find this across the board, this almost anthropomorphizing of AI. I remember one time I was in the car with my sister and brother-in-law and they were arguing over the instructions that came from the GPS.\nAnd the GPS happened to have a woman's voice. And they were saying, \"She said this or she said that\". And I said, \"Stop. It's not a she, it's an it\". But this relationship between the software and the individual, when you get to this\nvirtual world, it seems to be very, very tight and seems to be very hard to break and to get an understanding of here's humanity and here's the computer. Scott Hanselman : Yes. That's a whole podcast in itself. Pre-AI, the idea that we are\ntalking and yelling at these disembodied women in our houses, telling them to turn the lights off, whether it be Cortana or Alexa or any of these things, it's not a healthy thing at all. And as much as ChatGPT is kind of ruining everything,\nat least it's not given a name. At least it's not Jeff or Anna. I don't think that's healthy. But the problem is that if a parrot can speak, it becomes a much more interesting pet. When dogs do things like cock their eyebrows up\nor make expressions or smile, we love it. We ",

Create exactly three connected but intellectually distinct briefs. Piece 01 answers what changed and what matters. Piece 02 tests which claims withstand scrutiny and where evidence or interpretations are in tension. Piece 03 explains implications for the audience and what to watch next. Every claim needs at least one accepted exact-version reference. For written evidence, copy a short exact quote verbatim into locator.quote and set segment_id null. For media, use an existing segment_id and set quote null. Use at least three independent publishers per piece. Each piece needs a genuine inference connecting at least two publishers, an explicit reasoning chain in synthesis, and a meaningful limitation or counterargument. Distinguish reported facts, attributed source claims, and your own inference. Use claim IDs 01-c1, 01-c2, and so on.
